About the Town
SETTLED:
1718
INCORPORATED:
August 1, 1728
TYPE OF GOVERNMENT:
Town Meeting
LOCATION:
North Central Massachusetts, bordered by Townsend on the
north, Shirley on the east, Leominster and Lancaster on the
south, and Fitchburg and Ashby on the west. It is about 47
miles from Boston and 29 miles from Worcester.

Worcester

ANDHALL
NMINSER
WHER
BOARD OF SELECTMEN: Seated left to right - Ann P. Hall, Chairman August N. Misner and Walter B. Keeler. Standing
left to right - Executive Secretary Barry J. Stell and Town Counsel Timothy S. Hillman.
In recent years financial problems have dominated the tone and tenor of Town Government. Proposition 2½ -- while a
boon to the property owner -- has been the bane of local government officials. We have struggled with one another over a
smaller number of dollars and battled with the State for more assistance in order to provide the services of local government--
from education to snow-plowing. We are pleased to report that in Lunenburg the lines of communication are more open than
ever, and in most instances, the spirit of cooperation and respect for one another's problems and responsibilities is greater than
ever.
This spirit was manifested by the widespread support for education at the 1984 Annual Town Meeting and again by support
for the special needs of other departments at the November Special Town Meeting, notably by support for those faced with the
task of managing the problems of development in our community.
Growth is undoubtedly the major concern of Lunenburg for 1985. The robust economy under President Ronald Reagan has
opened the way for building and development; and our Town appears to be the darling of the developers. Responsible local gov-
ernment cannot deny building and so must MANAGE growth in a manner dedicated to retaining the desired quality of life in a
changing community.
As the Chief Executive of town government, the Board of Selectmen the duty to co-ordinate the planning and regulatory
activities of ALL town officials. Growth Management will require the financial support of those who must deliver supervision
and services in an eraof growth and change. The structure of town government must be equipped to meet the needs of the future.
Part-time officials and home grown common sense may have sufficed in the past, but the future must be met with the addition of
adequate facilities, professional management, and expertise if Lunenburg is to continue it's tradition of responsive, well-man-
aged local government.
REPORT OF EXECUTIVE SECRETARY TO THE BOARD OF SELECTMEN
The Board of Selectmen spent a great deal of 1984 confronting issues related to zoning. The increased complexity of zoning
was in proportion to the increase in zoning applications. In conjunction with zoning issues, the Board of Selectmen, serving as
Board of Health, began to take a close look at methods which would help protect the community's natural resources, specifically
its water supply. The Board of Selectmen committed themselves to drafting new Board of Health regulations which will
place tighter restrictions on the construction of additions on lots with limited square footage in order to regulate the effect of
construction on the use of the existing septic system and to determine the need for any improvements to the septic system.
The Board charged several new committees to look at specific issues that affect the residents of the community and town
government itself. A Cable Advisory Committee was appointed to advise the Board of Selectmen regarding theoperation of cable
television in the community. An Ad Hoc Communications Committee was formed resulting in a recommendation for a new and
modern phone system for Town Hall, the Highway Department and the Police and Fire Department complex. This phone system
became operable in August of 1984.
-4-
===PAGE 7===
Within the town government structure, a Safety Committee, consisting of representatives of various departments, was
formed for the specific purpose of improving safety conditions and thus reducing or eliminating accidents which result in loss
time in wages for employees and increased insurance premiums for the Town. A committee consistingof representatives of various
town departments and members of the community has also been developed to compile an assessment of the accessability to Town
programs and facilities for handicapped individuals. In addition, the Building Committee became active with new members who
spent long hours assessing the current status of the Town Hall facilities in order to ascertain what is needed to meet the current
and future needs of various Town Departments, Committees and Boards.
At the Annual Town Meeting in 1984, the Board sponsored an article to adopt Chapter 41, Section 23A of the Massach-
usetts General Laws which permits the Board of Selectmen to grant more authority and responsibility to the Executive Secretary.
This article was passed at the 1984 Annual Town Meeting.
The Office of Selectmen applied for two State grants in 1984. A feasibility study on the joint use of lock-upfacilities
by the City of Fitchburg and the towns of Ashburnham and Lunenburg was funded, and will determine adequate liability costs to
be shared by the communities and will hopefully result in a long term agreement regarding the use of the City of Fitchburg's lock-
up facilities by the Town of Lunenburg. In addition, a grant application was submitted to provide computer consultant services
for a feasibility study of the computerneeds of the town government and determine what coordination can be accomplished with
the current computer system within the School Department. The Board of Selectmen also signed an application for a grant from
the Clean Lakes Program. This application, sponsored by the residents of Lake Shirley, would provide a portion of the funds
needed to conduct an in depth feasibility study of the water quality of Lake Shirley and its watershed area, and develop recom-
mendations for methods to improve the current conditions.

Auditor
I have examined the financial statements for the fiscal year ended June 30, 1984. My examination included the books and
accounts of the Treasurer, Tax Collector, and all Committees entrusted with the receipt or expenditures of money, and all original
bills and vouchers on which money has been paid from the treasury. I have verified cash and reconciled the bank balances as
required, and in my opinion they present fairly the balances in these accounts (see Schedules 1, 2, and 3 attached). My exa-
minations were made in accordance with generally accepted auditing standards.
In my examination of all original bills and vouchers I also made tests for duplicate payments to certain vendors. No dis-
crepancies were found. All treasury warrants have been approved by the accounting officer. Audit testing disclosed number
of vouchers processed for payment without the proper approval of the appropriate department head. A standard approval form
has been initiated which has corrected the voucher approval process by the appropriate department head.
During my review of payroll, I found time sheets being processed without appropriate department head approval and instances
of sequential voided checks not being filed. A standard time sheet has been initiated by me and accepted by all department
heads and all checks are now being maintained and filed. Also, note was made of inconsistencies on pay for part-time employees
on holidays, and have been corrected by the Personnel Board.
Consistent with the practices of many municipalities in the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, the Town has not maintained
historical cost records of its' property, plant and equipment. I have, in a letter to the Selectmen, recommended a complete
inventory and cost up-date be initiated to provide current insurable values and eventually, fixed asset accounting in accordance
and conformity with generally accepted accounting principles. Neither property, plant and equipment, or materials and supplies
are capitalized at date of purchase, but are recorded as expenditures.
I submitted to the Selectmen a standard procedure for the settlement of insurance claims, and suggested that it be adopted
by the Town, for use by all departments. Some confusion occurred in the settlement of replacement for stolen property. It is
noted that all claims should be handled by one central control board, namely, the Board of Selectmen.
The Town of Lunenburg accepted the Uniform System of Accounting in 1930, and still uses its accounts systems. The Town
has adopted the revised 1981 Uniform Massachusetts Accounting System (U.M.A.S.) for its' financial statement presentation for
1982. The Department of Revenue has recommended the segregation of billing, collection and accounting functions for more
effective internal control. It is my opinion that safeguards exist, and that control is adequate.
This report completes my first fiscal year (F.Y. 1984) audit. It has been one of education, cooperation, initiating certain
corrective measures (as mentioned above). My examination foundother minor discrepancies which have been corrected. I certify
this report states my opinion of the accounting procedures in the Town of Lunenburg, and is correct to the best of my knowledge.

Board of Assessors
Board of Assessors: left to right - Secretary Jacqueline A. Keeler, William F. Reed, Bernard J. Paton and
Chairman Kathryn M. Herrick.
The year 1984 was a hectic one for the Assessors. The job of Assessors has become more complex and
time consuming over recent years. We feel that the work load, like that of the Building Inspector, has increased
to the point that our weekend field trips from September to February will no longer be sufficient to complete
the Job. The Assessors feel that now would be the best time to hire an Assistant Assessor and will include the
acquisition in the FY '86 budget request.
In FY '86 Lunenburg will be required to go through triennial recertification by the Department of Revenue
to show that we are assessing at "100% Full and Fair Market Value". This means that we will need a mini
revaluation. The firm of R. J. Finnegan Inc. was low bidder and chosen to do the real property portion. A. R. Gan-
non, Inc. was hired to do the personal property portion and both will begin in early January 1985.
In conjunction with the revaluation, a micro computer will be set up in the Assessor's Office. This will
enable the firm to complete their work more timely and also allow on-the-job computer training for assessors
and clerks. The Assessors will include an article in the Annual Town Meeting Warrant for the purchase of a
micro computer and associated software. We feel that this acquisition would greatly reduce the clerical work
load and eliminate outside contractural services which will result in a three year payback of funds required to
complete the process.
The tax rate for FY '85 was set at $20.70 per thousand with the total valuation of the Town $196,531,806.00.
The Board wishes to inform taxpayers that the following exemptions are available to those who qualify. If a tax-
payer feels that he qualifies he should contact the Assessor's Office to file the necessary application.
Clause 17C
- Widows, minors whose fathers are deceased, and persons over 70 - $2,000 in valuation
or a tax of $175, whichever is greater.
Clause 22
- Disabled or wounded veterans, or their wives, widows or parents - $2,000 to $10,000
valuation or a tax from $175 to $875, whichever is greater depending on degree of
disability.
Clause 22E
- Veterans with yearly certificate from Veterans' Administration indicating 100%
disability.
-8-
===PAGE 11===
Clause 37A
- Blind persons - $5,000 valuation or a tax of $437.50, whichever is greater.
Clause 41B
- Persons aged 70 or over - $4,000 valuation of a tax of $500, whichever is greater.
Clause 41A
- Tax deferral for certain persons 65 years of age or over with gross receipts of under
$20,000 annually to place the taxes on his domicile and one acre of land on a deferred
payment plan, the taxes to be paid upon settlement of the estate plus 8% interest.
Clause 42
- Unmarried widows of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000
valuation or a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Clause 43
- Minor orphans of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 valuation
or a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Chapter 61A
- Provides for assessment of agricultural or horticultural land at value based upon its
agricultural or horticultural use.
Chapter 61
- Provides for assessment of forest land managed under a planned program with certifi-
Chapter 61B
cation of State Forester.
Recreational Land
(1) Provides for assessment of land not less than five acres if it is retained in substan-
tially a natural, wild or open condition or in a landscaped condition in such a manner as
to allow to a significant extent the preservation of wildlife and other natural resources.
(2) Land deemed to be recreational land which is devoted primarily to recreational use
and is available to the general public or members of a non-profit organization.
Planning Board
Planning Board: left to right - George B. Larkin, Marion M. Benson, Paul J. Shattuck and Chairman James A.
Mann. Not present when photo was taken was Frederick L. Cuddy.
Since the acceptance by the Town of a new Master Plan in 1983, your Planning Board has set about in-
itiating its implementation. As we all recognize, Lunenburg is a desirable place in which to live, largely
because, in an area which is rapidly becoming suburbanized, Lunenburg has managed to maintain much of its
small town, semi-rural atmosphere. Recognizing that increased growth is inevitable, the Master Plan reflects
your concerns and wishes for orderly growth.
The variety of Planning Board articles in 1984, two special town meetings, the annual town meeting were
attempts to deal with some primary issues -
- keeping a zoned residential area, residential.
- incorporating accessory housing in a manner intended to assist current residents.
— initiated a survey of the town's water resources so as to enable sensible current land use development.
- initiated a town center study which will hopefully resolve most of the problems associated with population
growth which always carries with it an increased need for services - viz: fire, police, municipal administration,
recreation, education, etc.
-9-
===PAGE 12===
During 1984, 19 plans not requiring land sub-division approval were processed. Plans for the first Lunen-
burg lot in Pioneer Industrial Park were approved for Ecological Fibers of Clinton. The former Bigelow Estates
subdivision off Arbor Street, now Canterbury Drive, while initially approved, remains in limbo. There is at pre-
sent a preliminary plan for a sub-division off Island Road.
Numerous questions for zoning for cluster and condominium type housing developments have been
presented to the Board and are under consideration. It is the Board's consensus that such ordinances will
come about, but only after it has a better grasp on the best practical utilization of land in Lunenburg, l.e., water
availability, and the ability to safely dispose of waste waters.
Needless to say, much of the expertise needed for resolution of a number of the Board's questions does
not exist in-house. We are, therefore, seeking to obtain funds to retain the services of consultants as they are
needed. It is expected that this same consulting service will be useful to the needs of the Conservation Com-
mission as well. Not only do the two groups share common quarters, but have associated problems including,
until very recently, the availability of clerical assistance.
In closing, the Chairman would like to express his appreciation for the cooperation and effort put forth by
his Board - three of whom were new to the Board in the year 1984 and the fourth for approximately one year.
Library Trustees
Library Trustees: seated left to right - Carlton A. Wollenberg, Director Elizabeth H. Burnap and Chairman Ed-
ward A. O'Brien. Standing left to right - Kathleen M. Pliska, Jane Oliver-Purton and Jean M. Jones. Not pre-
sent when photo was taken was John P. Henshaw.
The year 1984 marked the arrival of Ritter Memorial Library's 75th anniversary year. As part of the "Old
Home Days" celebration in June, the laying of the library cornerstone was recalled with an open house and
ceremonies with trustees and town officials. The architect's original plans for the building were displayed, as
well as pictures and programs from earlier days. Among the guests were John and Alfred Gilchrest, who had at-
tended the actual cornerstone laying 75 years ago. Another event in the year long observance was a program
with Edith Thomas of Leominster demonstrating wall stenciling techniques and speaking on the history of
-10-
===PAGE 13===
stenciling. Other speakers are scheduled for the coming spring, and a grand finale in August, 1985, all funded
by a grant from the Massachusetts Arts Lottery. The trustees are sponsoring an art contest on the theme of
local history, with entries to be displayed through the spring and a prize awarded at the library rededication in
August.
Steadily increasing use of the library continues to stretch resources to the limit. Each year, the amounts
budgeted for basic on-going expenses must be held constant or reduced. Fewer books and other materials are
purchased. The building cannot be properly maintained and does not meet safety standards. Staff size is held
down even while absorbing growth in services and offering new ones, such as the circulation of videocasset-
tes supplied by the Central Massachusetts Regional Library System. A heavy, unexpected expense, now in the
offing, will be repairing the foundation, site of a serious leak. There is no money for this in the regular budget,
due to its already skeletal nature. Neither will there be funds for the usual professional spring cleaning or any
painting, projects hitherto accomplished with remaining year end funds.
Total circulation of materials for the year was 56,596 - 37.7% more than just five years ago. These items
were divided into the following categories: 22,157 adult, 1,070 young adult, and 33,369 children. Services for
children continue to be the fastest growing part of library operation. Accommodating four pre-school story
hour groups, pre-school movies, a summer reading program with 190 sign-ups and numerous special events,
the regular visits of all Primary School classes, and after school pupils from all grades requires skillful
organization, and continuous planning and supervision. A successful "Author Day" with Christopher Collier,
writer of children's historical fiction, was held in the Middle School, funded by the Massachusetts Arts Lottery.
The annual February Children's Art Festival was attended by an enthusiastic crowd.
The Friends of the Library have again provided invaluable support with their volunteeer work and funds for
various important items including: museum passes, story hour materials, summer programs, book awards for
the summer reading program, and rental books. They sponsored International Nights, speakers on investments
and calligraphy, and the Children's Art Festival.
Funds from the Lunenburg Memorial Fund, given in memory of Dr. James L. Congdon, were used to pur-
chase frames and poster size prints of photographs taken by Suzanne Blake and June Dettenrieder for an at-
tractive wall display. Chapter I Staff of Fitchburg donated a number of new books in memory of Mary Emery.
Books were also given in memory of Ronald Arnold, C. Cameron Burnap, and Mr. and Mrs. Ernest Tanguay.
Serious cuts in book buying were avoided through use of a substantial amount of library trust fund interest. A
bequest of $2,000 to the trust funds was received from the estate of Hazelle C. Kimball.
An intensive effort to recover long overdue books, including an amnesty from fines, brought a number of
volumes back to the shelves where they are once again available to the public. It was discovered that less than
1% of all materials checked out remain out indefinitely, indicating that there are relatively few truly irresponsi-
ble patrons.
Saturday hours have been changed from 1 p.m. - 5 p.m. to 10 a.m. - 2 p.m. which seems to please most
users.
A vacancy on the Board of Trustees, due to the resignation of June Dettenrieder, was filled in December by
the appointment of Jane Oliver-Purton to serve until the next town election.
Automation has now become a fact of life in large libraries and due to the growing interconnectedness of
all informational services, in smaller libraries as well. While the cost of computerizing library functions seems
prohibitive at present, it clearly will be a matter of "automate or stagnate" in the future. The trustees and staff
are investigating when and how this awesome step might be taken. At the same time, it must be borne in mind
that many individuals still want to use library resouces for recreational reading only, and their needs must be
considered before money is diverted from book buying to provision of automated information. All signs at this
time indicate serious problems ahead for the library in attempting to provide full services to users. The out-
come will depend simply on whether or not townspeople value their library enough to provide adequate sup-
port.
-11-
===PAGE 14===
Housing Authority
-
Housing Authority: Seated left to right - Judith K. Tarbell, Chairman Ella Rae Denault and Bessie H. Lindsay.
Standing left to right - Lance D. May and Executive Secretary, Eileen M. Alleva. Not present when photo was
taken was Josephine C. Melloon.
The Lunenburg Housing Authority is in its 15th year in the operation of the facilities at 131 White Street,
known as the Pearl Brook Housing for the Elderly. Presently, we have forty-five apartments occupied by single
persons, and three occupied by couples.
The tenants have organized the Pearl Brook Tenants' Association which meets on a regular basis and ar-
ranges Interesting projects for the benefit and enjoyment of its members. Mr. Eugene DesLauriers holds the of-
fice of President. He is the principal liaison between the tenants and the Authority members.
The tenants are provided with weekly transportation by the Council on Aging, and the shut-ins are provid-
ed with "Meals on Wheels". The Nashoba Associated Boards of Health provide monthly Well-Oldster Clinics
for the tenants. Home Health Care provides household duties and shopping. Holy Mass is celebrated every
Monday at 11:00 A.M.
The requirements for occupancy are that the applicants be at least sixty-five years of age, and in need of
good housing. Rents are variable in that no more than 25% of income can be charged for rent which includes
heat, water, and electricity. The rising cost of energy makes it very difficult for the Authority to keep within
their budget.
We invite anyone who is interested to inquire at the Housing Authority Office at 131 White Street, where
Mrs. Eileen Alleva, Executive Secretary, will be available to answer questions. You may call 345-7655 or 342-
5308.
Park Commission
The year 1984 was a period of rehabilitation for parks and recreation. For the first time in five years money
was added to the Town's recreational activities and much needed maintenance of facilities was performed.
Our plan is to continue to enhance our recreational programs as well as restore the facilities. In order to ac-
complish this, we asked all Park employees to forego their annual salary increase (4%) so that the money could
be channeled into programs and maintenance. This dedication and community spirit on behalf of all those
-12-
===PAGE 15===
employed by the Park is the strength of our community. Each deserves a special thanks not only for their
sacrifice but for the continued effort to improve our community. We would also like to acknowledge the con-
tributions by those who volunteered their time, money and supplies, for without them our budget would be
20% higher.
A review of the programs offered: The town beach under Jerry Beloin enjoyed an excellent year. A ques-
tionnaire about the town beach, the beach house and its continued viability was overwhelmingly affirmed. The
swimming instruction programs were well attended. The town beach continued to be one of the most widely
used facilities. The summer recreation program headed by Dick Stachowicz enjoyed a banner year for par-
ticipation by our youth. The playgrounds, arts and crafts, summer basketball and men's softball provided a
diverse mix of recreational activities. Some of the highlights of the summer recreation program were all day
trips to Canobie Lake and Salisbury Beach, watermelon and soda hunts and a junior track meet for ages 6 to 13
which had over 100 participants.
An extensive Winter Basketball Program consisted of the Lunenburg Junior Basketball League, Sun-
day Men's League and Teen League (both under the direction of Ron Lamothe). There also was a Men's Friday
Open Gym and a Saturday Afternoon Open Gym for Grades 4-12. The Lunenburg Junior Basketball League add-
ed a girls' division with four teams and 43 girls participating. Over 200 children participated in the Junior
Basketball League. Almost 375 players and coaches were involved in the entire winter basketball program.
For the coming year we are continuing to spruce up our playground areas, as well as repair and replace
worn or broken recreational equipment. We also would like to reinstitute the summer instructional tennis pro-
gram. The arts and crafts program for the children will be upgraded.
In closing, we must thank Tom Leppala who is charged with upkeep and maintenance of all the facilities.
During the spring he must prepare six fields to handle seven games a day. Without his dedication, the Park
Commission could not function.
Members of the Park Commission are Chairman Joseph A. Giovino, Martha M. Johnson and Robert A.
Membrino.

Personnel Board
Personnel Board: Seated Lynn F. Markham. Standing left to right - Michael V. Sack, Beatrice E. Stepton and
Chairman, John L. Plank, Jr. Not present when photo was taken was Richard E. Baker.
The Personnel Board continues to find new challenges in dealing with Town personnel. The ulitmate
responsibility of the Board is to ensure that both the Town and its employees are treated equitably and fairly.
Town employees are encouraged to ask questions and air complaints with the Board.
Because of the Town's budgetary procedure, the Board must decide very early in the budget process just
what increase there will be in the Salary Administration Plan. This puts the Board and the Town employees at a
disadvantage because other groups settle just before or after Town Meeting. If there is a difference in the
percentage increase between the various groups, then the Board must play "catch up" at later Town Meetings.
The Personnel Board wishes the situation were different, but as our Town budget procedure now stands, we
see this "catch up" process as being a fact of life.
The growth of Lunenburg and new government regulations In recent years has added a new dimension to
the scope of the Board's responsiblity. As other boards see their workload increase, we are adding higher level
employees to our plan to aid the Town in handling the increased responsibilities.
Lunenburg is fortunate to have fine, dedicated employees and the Personnel Board intends to keep them
enthusiastic and dedicated through fair treatment and prompt attention to their problems.

Animal Control Officer
The year 1984 was a very productive and busy
one for your Animal Control Officer. My office receiv-
ed approximately 1,650 calls and responded to them
either by telephone or in person. Calls needing my
personal attention for any reason were responded to
promptly. Also, I had many other kinds of calls in-
volving homing pigeons, falcons, donkeys, cows,
horses, pigs, turkeys, raccoons, skunks, opossum,
goats and sheep.
Due to legislative changes, we are now able to
generate money in larger sums in this Department
because we are completely independent from the
County. Our fees were raised at the Special Town
Meeting, and the most important thing I accomplish-
ed was finally achieving sexual equality for our dogs.
It is now one fee for females and males and a lower
price for neutered and spayed dogs. This hopefully
will give people the push needed to fix their animals
and hopefully will cut down on our stray population
and on unwanted pupples being born and abandon-
ed. We had 180 strays this year, 15 went back to their
original homes, 30 were "euthanized" because of
health or vicious disposition and 135 were placed
with either the Hearing Ear Dog Program, private
homes, or dog handling programs.

Conservation Commission
Conservation Commission - Seated left to right - Glenn S. Stillman and Matthew E. Pearson. Standing left to
right - Thomas W. Lawson and Russell N. Harvey, Jr. Not present when photo was taken were Chairman, Nan-
cy I. Sholl, Robert C. Leupold and Lee DeNike.
The Conservation Commission wishes to extend its gratitude to the Board of Selectmen, the Board of
Health, and the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health for their cooperation during the past year. This coopera-
tion was especially appreciated considering the fact that the Commission has added four new members.
These include Russell Harvey, Matthew Pearson, Thomas Lawson, and Lee DeNike.
The Commission regretfully acknowledges the resignations of former Chairman, Raymond Chapdelaine
and Members, John Lahti and Robert Garneau. Their years of sage guidance have resulted in a legacy of protec-
tion for the Town's wetland areas.
Historically, the task of the Commission has been one of managing and protecting the Town's con-
servation lands. While attempting to continue to do this, the emphasis of the work of the Commission has
dramatically changed during the past year because of legislative mandates and the continued building growth
within the Town. Through the Commonwealth's Wetlands Act, the Commission is charged with the respon-
sibility of protecting the Town's wetlands. This responsibility has resulted this year in ten hearings, twenty-
seven inspections, and two cease and desist orders. This unanticipated but important role has led members of
the Commission to temporarily defer their goals of obtaining more conservation land and acquainting the
Townspeople with the existing holdings.
One of the potential critical problems facing the Town is that of contaminates leaking from underground
storage facilities. Such a condition would pose a threat to the environment and to the public health. To prevent
such damage, the Commission solicits the support of the citizens to enact safeguards.
-17-
===PAGE 20===
Town Forest Committee
The Town Forest Committee has concluded its
study of the Town Forest off Gilchrest Street. We
have decided, with the aid of Forest Consultant,
Robert Leupold, to begin a long term management
plan. The plan calls for systematic thinning of crowd-
ed growth and removal of poor or diseased trees. The
plan is based on 10 year rotation cuttings or thinn-
ings aimed at creating a productive and mature
forest.
Mr. Leupold has marked trees for cutting in two
plots. Total cordwood is calculated at 220 cords. No
timber or sawlogs are available as of yet. Mr. Leupold
put out to bid and showed plots this Fall. Presently,
it appears that we will be accepting one proposal for
both plots.
The development and implementation of the
management plan has taken more time than ex-
pected. This is due to the fact that it had to be
created from scratch. The managed forest will over a
period of time present an Interesting and educa-
tional contrast to other Town owned properties. The

Highway Department
The Highway Users Federation warns that sub-
tle population shifts are changing transportation
problems dramatically, and that these changes are
not being addressed in national transportation plan-
ning.
The traditional country-to-city migration pattern
has been reversed in selected areas as people follow
jobs and housing to the outlying fringes of
metropolitan areas. This movement is creating traf-
fic congestion in unlikely places, as people and jobs
locate in what were once relatively remote com-
munities, notes the Federation.
There are no national plans to relieve growing.
congestion in scattered rural areas or in suburbs, the
two areas where most Americans now travel daily. In-
stead, national transportation policy is aimed largely
at the problems of central cities, particularly the
commute to downtown, the Federation reports. The
census clearly shows that downtown is the work
destination of a declining proportion of Americans,
even urban Americans. In urban areas, commuting is
growing fastest within and between suburbs.
According to the Federation, as a nation, we can
choose to accept a future of steadily worsening traf-
fic congestion in our fastest growing places, or we
can develop a forward-looking highway program -
as a previous generation did with the interstate
highway concept - designed to handle the chang-
George G. Munyon, Jr., Highway Superintendent
ing travel needs of a dynamic, vital society both safe-
ly and efficiently. (Above is excerpt from American
allotments until July, which is the beginning of the
City & Country Magazine.)

money was spent under either State allotment.

Council on Aging
Seated left to right - Sandra P. Flanagan, Doris M. Carrier, Elvira Z. May and Martha L. Mologhan. Standing left
to right - Alfred C. Gilchrest, William A. Dahlgren, Rodney C. Brown, Chairman John E. Sandberg and Chester
J. Pliska. Not present when photo was taken were Edward J. Moran and Lucienne Chapdelaine.
This has been another year of progress in several areas of expanding service and maintaining our program
in other areas. The first area of expanding service is our Wednesday Morning Drop-In Center. At the beginning
of 1985, we moved the Center from the United Parish building to St. Boniface Church where a larger room and
easier parking and access awaited us. We now have room for many more people to attend. Dorls Lahti and her
friends assist in maintaining supplies. Our Transportation Program continues to grow in usage. This program
provides transportation to and from area shopping centers, and medical and service facilities at a one dollar
per round trip cost. This service is offered on Tuesdays from 10 A.M. until 4 P.M. and on Thursdays from 9 A.M.
until 1 P.M. The special van that accommodates the handicapped is available anytime at the same rate - one
dollar per round trip. We are pleased with the personal service that Elnard Warpula and his staff provide. Bill
Dahlgren also assists in helping the ill or handicapped in this program.
The Hot Meals Program for those who need a good meal at home, but are unable to prepare it for
themselves, continues to expand as the need for this service continues to grow. Our experience in the last few
months shows an average of about 14 meals per day. Finding drivers to deliver these meals is quite a
challenge. The price we pay for these meals is rising again. We will begin to pay $2.50 per meal on July 1, 1985,
and we ask recipients to pay $1.00 if they are able. Special thanks to Sandra Flanagan and Martha Mologhan
who oversee the program and to those very special drivers.
Other programs such as the Christmas Dinner, Newletter and Bus Trips continue on with minor changes.
In a number of these areas we cooperate with the "Friendly Seniors", a separate and voluntary organization,
and we seek to be mutually helpful.
The one development that speaks well for better service in all areas would be the hiring of a part-time
secretary for the Department. This person would have office space, a file cabinet, typewriter and desk in a
small office in the United Parish Building.
During the year Helen White resigned from the Council and her place has been filled by Rodney Brown. By
last count there are 1, 168 Seniors (those 60 years or older) in our town. They are a splendid group - energetic,
care for one another, and enjoy doing many things together.

Inspector of Wires
During 1984, the office of Wiring Inspector
maintained a high level of activity. In conjunction
with the nationwide trend of a "boom" in con-
struction, the Town of Lunenburg experienced un-
precedented growth in new home construction, addi-
tions to existing structures, as well as new commer-
cial ventures which were undertaken.
Typically, any new structure or addition to a
structure would require the installation of wiring. To
conform with the law, any wiring must be accom-
panied by a wiring permit and receive the proper in-
spections. Other examples of wiring which require a
permit would be service changes, temporary ser-
vices, and swimming pools. All wiring must conform
to the latest edition of the Massachusetts Electric
Code.
In 1984, this office issued approximately 150
wiring permits which resulted in 360 inspections.
These figures are the highest they have been in my
tenure as Wiring Inspector. A fee is required for all
wiring permits and inspections. The permits are
issued at the Town Clerk's office and current fee
schedules are available from that office.
At this time I would like to thank the Building In-
spector, Plumbing Inspector, Fire Chief, and the
Town Clerk's office for their assistance and coopera-
tion during the year.
James R. Sharkey, Wiring Inspector
Inspector of Plumbing & Gas Fitting
The year 1984 was a very active one for the In-
spector of Plumbing and Gas Fittings. There were 39
plumbing permits issued for new homes, 18 for
alterations/remodeling, 9 for commercial buildings
and 2 for solar hot water installations. The issuance
of these permits resulted in 146 plumbing in-
spections and, in some cases, the eventual issuance
of an occupancy permit by the Building Inspector.
Let us not forget the essential purpose of the Plumb-
ing Code is to safeguard the potable water supply
and to provide for safe and efficient disposal of
human waste products.
There were 50 gas fitting permits issued in 1984
- 49 for various gas appliances and 1 for a com-
mercial building. Natural gas as an energy fuel is still
in big demand in the area, but the lack of gas main
extensions and the cost of new home services,
which are now being paid for by the customer, has
slowed the installation of gas appliances to some
extent in 1984. Two Gas Code violations were
reported - one required a hearing, the other was
Thomas E. Mortis
resolved without legal action.

Historical Commission
Historical Commission: left to right - William E.
were Chairman Carolyn W. Simsarian and Mary Ann
Perkins, Dorothy A. Hillman, Marian L. Miller and An-
Sudolnik.
nabelle Perkins. Not present when photo was taken
This has been a year of orientation and
reorganization for the Historical Commission. With a
full slate of new members, the Commission is once
again taking up the task of an inventory of our
historical assets. The purpose of the Historical Com-
mission is to preserve the past for future genera-
tions. The inventory is the first step in documenting
the historical assets of the Town. Included in the in-
ventory are areas, buildings, structures and sites
which are historically, architecturally, archeological-
ly or culturally significant. The inventory contains
the exact location and a statement of its
significance to the community with appropriate
documentation. Once completed, the inventory is
sent to the Massachusetts Historical Commission.
Through the Massachusetts Historical Commission,
application could then be made for such things as in-
clusion on the National Register of Historic Places,
consideration for federal and state assisted pro-
grams, federal tax benefits, and other programs to
assist in the maintaining of our historic structures
and sites.
Lunenburg is a town with a rich and colorful
history. We are fortunate to be a town with residents
interested in preserving that history. The Historical
Commission wishes to continue this tradition. The
Commission stands ready to assist in any way with
other committees in Town who are involved in plann-
ing for the Town's future. It is imperative that as we
plan for the future, we are cognizant of the ir-
replaceable historic value and significance of our
Town and its structures, and incorporate into our
plannning that which preserves the past as well as
meets current and future needs.
Lunenburg Town Hall

Building Inspector
and increased secretarial time to 30 hours per week.
The Building Department will be consolidated and
shall have increased responsibilities and workload.
This increase is in response to demands imposed by
the increases in building permit issuance, zoning
problems, and inspections. The increase in housing
for Massachusetts as shown in the 1984 2nd and 3rd
quarter Dodge Survey Reports, indicates the average
of a 24% growth.
One of the most Important things to be aware of
is the time demand placed upon this Department as
a result of the type of permit issuance that is taking
place. For example, wood stove permits usually re-
quire one inspection. New dwellings, additions and
commercial work require several inspections, in-
creasing in multiples of five or more for a new dwell-
ing, to as many as ten for a commercial project. For
example, one permit for a new dwelling could mean
five Inspections, two permits mean ten inspections,
etc. Compounded with other permit inspections, this
increases the demand for inspections as more and
more permits are being issued. Also, each permit re-
quires plan review, thus creating even more time
demands. Building permits issued in Lunenburg dur-
ing 1984 are as follows:

Town Management Study Committee
Town Management Study Committee: Seated left to right - Walter B. Keeler and Melissa D. Cobb. Standing
left to right - Gail R. Wolejko, John C. Floros and Diane J. Allen
It was voted at the last Annual Town Meeting to continue the Town Management Study Committee for the
year 1984-1985. Two new members, Gail Wolejko and John Floros, joined the Committee filling vacancies made
by the resignations of James Rockefeller and Herb Dunnington. We thank the former members for their
valuable contributions and welcome the new.
Based upon the recommendations of the 1983-1984 Study Committee, Chapter 41, Section 23A of the
General Laws was adopted at the 1984 Town Meeting, thus establishing the position of Executive Secretary.
The appointment of Barry Stell to this position has enabled the Board of Selectmen to delegate any of their
lawful duties to him. Indications are that this has helped ease the burden on the Selectmen. The Executive
Secretary organizes agendas and background information for Selectmen's meetings. He also acts as the
Board's agent in several areas including zoning; he is a llaison to various departments; prepares specifications
for bids and legal notices, etc. All this is done under the authority and supervision of the Board of Selectmen.
The focus of our continuing study has been to identify the changes which have taken place since the
adoption of Chapter 41, Section 23A and to gather more data specific to the Town of Lunenburg. In order to
clearly define problem areas, we have circulated questionnaires and solicited information from Town depart-
ments and officials regarding the problems and assets of our current form of government. We wish to thank
those who have responded. We expect by the Annual Town Meeting of 1985 to have a more definite outcome of
the questionnaires and our recommendations for 1986.
-32-
===PAGE 35===
Town Water Study Committee
Water Study Committee: Seated left to right - George B. Larkin, Scott W. Foster and Russell N. Harvey, Jr.
Standing left to right - Donald Gillis, James L. Deming, James A. Mann and Arlen D. White.
The Water Study Committee began meeting in the fall of 1984. Its objective is to form a strategy for identi-
fying and protecting new sources of drinking water. The Committee unanimously believes the need exists,
especially when viewed against future population growth.
Four potential sources have been discussed: (1) Buying water from a neighboring community; (2) Use an
existing surface water supply such as a lake; (3) Develop additional water bearing sand and gravel deposits
within the Town; and (4) Develop bedrock aquifer within the Town. The first and second alternatives were given
low priority because of political, cost and/or lack of control problems. The third alternative has the highest
priority because it is the least expensive and is a proven approach. The fourth alternative has great potential,
but is not allowed by the State unless the third approach has been fully researched.
The following are the Committee's recommendations
1.
View the situation regarding potable water not just as of today, but also for the longer
time span of 50 or 100 years into the future. We should develop an action plan as a
community focusing on long term health and independence. Water is critical to both.
2.
The Town of Lunenburg should consider this its problem. There is a present problem.
It does not stop with the Water District, but includes areas beyond the District's con-
fines and financial capacity.
3.
The future should be secured with new additional water sources, beyond the water-
shed of the present Town wellfield. Testing should be continued to Identify such
sources. Once identification is completed, the Town should acquire In fee Title the
primary source area if not a larger land area. Two, three or more sources should be set
aside and protected for the long term well being of the community.
-33-
===PAGE 36===
Montachusett Regional Vocational
Technical School District
The year 1984 has been marked by significant accomplishments for Montachusett Regional Vocational
Technical School. When the Class of 1984 graduated in June, 92.2 percent had made a decision about their
future, and were already placed in a job, signed up for military service, or had opted for further education.
(These figures are based on a survey of 180 returns out of 200 graduates.)
A new course offered to staff for the first time In 1984 was an after-hours computer class, using Apple IIE
and Atari 800 computers. When the second session is completed in 1985, 48 staff members will have expanded
their computer knowledge. Thomas J. Shaughnessy, Director of Guidance, received the Outstanding Public
Employee Award from the state, and was recognized in a special ceremony in June, 1984. Monty Tech was
again the site of the Fitchburg conference, an annual professional improvement week of seminars and
workshops.
Students recorded academic and vocational achievements. "Eighty-four Odyssey", the yearbook, received
a Meritorious Achievement Award from the New England Scholastic Press Association. Students had ex-
cellent results in VICA (Vocational Industrial Clubs of America) competitions, earning one gold, two silver, and
one bronze medal. Students were active in the National Honor Society, S.A.D.D., Student Council, and the Stu-
dent Advisory Council. Parental interest resulted in the establishment of the Parents Advisory Committee,
which publishes an informative newsletter about the school, and provides volunteers in several school offices.
Academic programs continued to change, expand, and improve. The Monty Tech academic program matches
any in the area, and students who meet the expectations of the program will more than meet requirements of
any further education they may pursue.
Lunenburg's representative to the District is Charles F. Valera.
Finance Committee: Seated left to right - William C. Bartlett, Paula J. Fletcher and Chairman Richard M. Mills.
Standing left to right - Roy Hall III and Arno J. Hangas. Not present when photo was taken were F. Clifton
Miller and Marie A. Foppiano.

TOWN CLERK, TAX COLLECTOR AND TREASURER
I am pleased to submit my second annual report. The year 1984 has
proved to be a very busy year, which, through both education and
experience, has resulted in improved operations.
As can be seen from the first page of this report, a hallmark of my
administration has been to fully communicate with the townspeople.
It is the responsibility of all public officials to report on a
regular basis what they have done to better the town. This allows
for feedback SO that voters have input between Town Meetings.
Much has been studied and discussed about ways to improve town
government. We are not faced with a crisis of management, rather
we are faced with a crisis of communication. Changing the form of
government may not enhance the exchange of information if there
are still problems. We must solve the obvious problems, rather
than trying to solve manufactured ones. There must be a free
flowing communication without distrust on the part of any depart-
ment. One solution is a monthly networking session of all Town
department heads, with each, on a rotating basis, presenting a
program on how it works.
In order to serve the townspeople more conveniently, the three
offices are now open on Thursday nights until 7 p.m. This coincides
with the hours of Lunenburg banks, and will help those who work out
of town to conduct town business.
TREASURER
Robert J. Ebersole
MONTY TECH, ROUND 1
The Town is faced with many assessments over which it has little direct control, such as the Worcester County
Tax or Retirement System. The Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is another such assessment.
If 2/3 or more of the member communities vote the submitted budget, it passes over the objections of those
opposed.
Monty Tech is outside of the restraints of Proposition 2½, and can increase its assessments at a greater rate
that we can increase our taxes. Due to changes in state funding in 1984, "pass through" aid to cites and
towns stayed with regional schools. This money, plus underestimated state aid, were not included when the
FY 1985 budget was presented. Over $850,000 was involved.
As Treasurer, I sent out letters of Finance Committees, Mayors, Selectmen and Monty Tech representatives of
member communities to alert them of this situation. Through much media exposure, attendance at public hearings
and many phone calls to pressure for a favorable vote, we were successful.
The Monty Tech School Board voted to return $500,000 in April, and further reduced the assessments even more
than originally requested. This is in part due to a state audit which ordered a reduction in the amount kept
in the school's various accounts. What was the final effect? Lunenburg's original assessment estimate of
$85,253 is now $42,366, which is over a 50% cut.
LUNENBURG RATES AN "A"
Moodys Investors Service provides bond ratings for states, cities and towns throughout the United States.
This bond rating comes into play for major borrowing needs. There are many factors which affect ratings. Many
are beyond the control of a municipality, such as the general economic climate of a state, national growth
(or decline), or state-wide restrictions on the ability to tax (such as Proposition 2½).
However, the financial operations of the Town do play an important part in the process. Does the Town pay its
bills on time? Is there reliance on short-term borrowing to finance current operations? Does the Town have
a reasonable debt level? In other words, does Lunenburg pay its way without borrowing against the future?
Prop. 2½ was not looked upon favorably by Moodys because it impaired a town's ability to pay debt as well as
current expenses. Since Lunenburg has voted to exempt the fire truck debt service, it will help maintain our
rating because it shows that we are willing to tax ourselves to finance needed capital projects.
Another important aspect to the rating is control over growth of budgets. According to Moodys Bond Survey
dated 2/11/85, "The ability to successfully exert expenditure controls appears for many municipalities a
difficult objective to achieve.' Although the current financial situation is better than most had forecasted,
spending on non-capital items should not be increased at the expense of badly needed capital items such as
-36-
===PAGE 39===
roads, public buildings and equipment. For example, once new employees are added into budgets, those positions
will probably never be eliminated thereby impairing the capital budgeting process.
As has been stated year after year, the amount of "Free Cash" available at the end of a fiscal year is
considered to be an important indicator of financial health. Between 3 and 5% of the town's budget is what
Moodys considers reasonable. For Lunenburg, this would be between $230,000 and $390,000. A major source of
free cash is that amount which is not used during one year, and carried over to the next. As the economy cools,
we will need to keep more free cash because state aid and local receipts will decrease. We should therefore
resist the temptation to once more draw it down to dangerous levels.
COMPUTERS
Under Prop. 2½, state mandated procedures which are deemed "incidental" by the state do not need to be funded
by the legislature. Many agencies are requiring more and more reports, which entail more record keeping than
currently in place. These procedures would be greatly simplified if the office was computerized.
TAX COLLECTOR
The Tax Collector is perhaps the least liked officer in the Town. No one really likes paying taxes, but if
the collection process is administered fairly and consistently, all can be assured of equal treatment. The
State has mandated many procedures to insure that taxes will be collected. All bills are counted, amounts
totaled and an affidavit of mailing is filed with the Department of Revenue. The responsibility for payment
is upon the taxpayer, whether a bill is received or not.
Town cash flow considerations require that the collector be ever vigilant. We were able to do the difficult
work to attain our goal of 100% collection rate for fiscal year 1984 real estate taxes. This meant that
either the taxes were paid, or the property was placed into tax title, thereby protecting the town's interest
in the taxes due.
The process to collect fiscal year 1985 real estate taxes began with the May Annual Town Meeting, where the
amount to be raised was determined. We were 5th in the state to have the tax rate set, due to cooperation
between the Treasurer-Collectors' Assessors' and Selectmen's offices. This allowed my office to process the
bills, mail them on time, and receive payments SO that the Town did not have to borrow for short term uses.
As an interesting side note, most people do not realize that we send over 10,000 motor vehicle excise bills a
year. Eliminating transfer registrations, there are approximately 8000 vehicles in Town. It is easy to see
how traffic problems occur.
TOWN CLERK
ELECTIONS, ELECTIONS, ELECTIONS
It is clear that 1984 was a year of contrasts. Over 5000 people were registered to vote for the Presidential
Election, yet we had the lowest turnout in over 30 years of the Annual Town Election. Turnout depends on the
contests and issues involved - the November election was SO important to people that a record 4211 voted,
while there were no contests in the Town Election except for the debt exclusion for the fire truck. The
Presidential Preference Primary was held on a snowy day in March, reducing the vote to only 738.
Throughout the year, major efforts were made to assure that all could register to vote if they were eligible.
Registrations sessions were held at the High School, Fitchburg State College, Mt. Wachusett Community College,
as well as weekend and evening sessions.
To speed returns of elections, votingmachines are part of the Town's capital budget plan. Over the years, we
have been able to find hardworking and talented people to hand count ballots, and the Town can be grateful to
them for their quality work and the spense saved.
RECORDING OUR WORDS FOR POSTERITY
For the first time in Lunenburg's history, the special and annual town meetings held in 1984 were tape
recorded. Authorized by state law, the recordings will provide a back-up to the manual minutes process, and
will be an important addition to the Town's historical records. The tapes may be consulted to look behind a
vote in the event that there is a contest or lawsuit as to the purpose of a particular bylaw or appropriation.
These tapes are a public record, and are accessible during regular office hours.
HISTORICAL PRESERVATION
The Town Clerk is charged with the safekeeping of the Town's records. Based on this duty, a grant was
applied for, and received from, the Massachusetts Council on the Arts and Humanities to survey our records.
The Northeast Document Conservation Center in Andover performed the work by reviewing the records, their
storage and access, as well as building and environmental conditions.
The 22 page report detailed simple and complex solutions to the current situation. Some of the changes will
depend on what the Town decides as to the fate of the Town Hall - renovation or building a new structure. The
records are threatened by light, dust, handling, mold, moisture, heat, fire, vandalism and rodents.
-37-
===PAGE 40===
The information in the records is used on a regular
basis in the current administration of the Town, and
provides an important historical context to our
operation. The cornerstone of this preservation effort
is for the restoration and microfilming of old records.
We currently do not have a copy of most records. Once
they are microfilmed, the originals will be put into
safekeeping, a copy placed in our safe deposit box, and
the the microfilm can be used for our day to day needs.
LUNENBURG LIVE!
Since the Town Clerk's Office is the clearinghouse for
much of the information of what goes on in town, it was
a natural extension that the Clerk be the talk show host
on local TV. Airing on the 4th Thursday of each month
on Montachusett Cable TV, Channel 3, the show has
highlighted town organizations and departments of
interest to residents, a community calendar, high school
sports, as well as works of local artists. Any group
interested in being a guest should contact the Clerk's
office.

===PAGE 66===
Vital Statistics
Following are those records of Births, Marriages, and Deaths that either occured in our Town or records
which were forwarded to this Town from other municipalities in the State. Lunenburg residents are urged to
notify the Town Clerk if In-State records have not been received and recorded. If a Birth, Marriage, or Death OC-
curred outside the State, the same may be recorded in the Lunenburg Town Clerk's office if certified records
are presented. In some cases the Town Clerk's records are supplemented by records of burials which appear
elsewhere in this report. In order for these records to also be Included in the official Vital Statistics records, the
family of the deceased should present a certified copy of the death certificate to the Town Clerk. In accordance
with recent changes in the General Laws marriages which occur here but relate to licenses which were obtain-
ed elsewhere in the State are no longer included in our official Town Records.

Annual Report of
Superintendent of Schools
It is a privilege to submit my seventh annual report
as Superintendent of Schools in Lunenburg. This annual
report covers the year ending December 31, 1984.
As you read the reports of the other administrators,
you will receive a more detailed accounting of the events
that have taken place in the schools. The purpose of this
report is to summarize the total aspects of the Lunenburg
Public Schools.
WHERE ARE WE NOW?
I feel that this question needs to be somewhat held as
the issue of the year. Most citizens have their interpre-
tation of the answer to this question. Most teachers, stu-
dents and parents can give an assessment of the situation.
The school committee members, who are exposed to more
information and documentation than anyone beside theSuper-
intendent of Schools, have their own assessment. As the
WILLIAM C. ALLARD, Ed. D.
chief executive officer of the Lunenburg Public Schools, I
Superintendent of Schools
must be in a position to explain and answer this question
to all of the citizens in Lunenburg.
We have come through some very difficult times. The sudden cut back of monies available to the school
department after the passage of Proposition 21 required us in a very short period of time to lay off many
staff members and cut our expenses. We were not afforded the luxury of time to affect these changes and con-
sequently it has taken time and effort to recapture a number of those losses. This has been accomplished by
a slow and deliberate process. Over those years we have made an attempt to improve our programs, update our
staff through inservice courses, apply for and receive a number of federal and state grants, introduce new
programs and seek out the most qualified staff available for any position that became open. All of these
efforts have resulted in placing us here today. I could write a long litany of programs and needs that we
have addressed over the years to indicate our relative position of strength. Suffice it to say that we have
what we have today because of the efforts of everyone.
We are a quality school system. I say that because all evidence used to indicate this has been applied
to us. For example, our graduates do get accepted to excellent schools. All state reports filed indicate
that our students score well above average on their work. Our daily attendance to our schools is on the
average 95%-96%. Our individual programs bring credit to our schools and our students. Our athletic teams
are well staffed with students and they are well coached. We are in compliance with all mandates from the
State Board, of Education and we have received commendations on our special education programs, basic skills
programs and our financial accounting. We feel that we provide many opportunities for student, parent, staff
and community input. Organizations such as L-CAN, SADD, Principals' Advisory Committees, student organiza-
tions, Lunenburg Education Association, Lunenburg Public Schools Community Relations Committee are available
for input and discussions. The bottom line is that we do have an outstanding staff of teachers, principals,
secretaries, custodians, food service personnel available on a daily basis to service the needs of the stu-
dents, parents and citizens of this community.
We are now, however, just short of making this one of the top school systems in the state. We need more
staff to implement programs that are either missing or partially implemented. I am referring to additional
staff in the music department, libraries, foreign language and science areas. We also need to increase the
funding of our athletic budgets to remove the burden of fundraising from the booster clubs. These are a few
of the items that address the issue of providing more for our young people. We, in administration, will
attempt to provide for an increased growth and accountability for our programs.
JANUARY 1984 - JUNE 1984:
Although we had a successful year in completing our academic school year, this was a particularly
difficult time because of a very tight budget. As you will recall, we received an increase of 4.3% over the
previous year to run the schools. Since this operation is highly labor intensified, we made the decision to
cut the expense portion of our budget by $124,000. over the previous year's expenditures. Those cuts show
up during the year, but they become very noticeable by the year's end. However, in spite of our financial
difficulties, we completed the year quite well. The school department finished the year with all bills
paid and in compliance with all state mandates. I would dare say, however, that we did end the year with
our cupboards bare!
Much of the administrators and school committee time during this period was spent in preparing for the
FY-85 budget presentation to the Finance Committee and to the town meeting. This presentation was based on
-91-
===PAGE 94===
the fact that the present FY-84 budget was not adequate to support a quality educational system. It was
obvious to all who had any knowledge about the school system that we needed a great financial boost in the
expense portion of our budget. We also needed to get on with a proper maintenance program for our
facilities.
At the annual town meeting in May 1984, the town voted a budget increase of 10.4% in the school budget.
In addition, we received support for two articles. One article supported $70,000. for the repairs to the
high school roof and the other supported $31,000. for installation of a wheelchair lift to be installed at
the high school. We ended the school year making plans and preparations for the new school year.
SEPTEMBER 1984 - DECEMBER 1984:
After the town meeting in May, we immediately set out to provide this town with the proper staffing for
the opening of school in September. In addition, we proceeded to purchase the necessary materials, text-
books, and equipment purchases necessary for the opening of school. Furthermore, we went out to bid for the
continuation of the roof repair at the high school and also sought bids for the wheelchair lift to be in-
stalled at the high school. We worked very diligently to seek out the best available staff to fill new
positions or replacements for existing positions. By September, we were prepared for the opening of the
school year buoyed by the increase in the budget and an opportunity to put this school system back on the
direction of reclaiming some of the quality it had lost over the past few years.
WHAT DID A 10.4% INCREASE IN THE LUNENBURG PUBLIC SCHOOLS BUDGET ATTAIN?
In this portion I will attempt to provide the reader with an overview of what has been accomplished
with a total budget of $4,367,500., an increase of 10.4% over the previous FY-84 budget. Let me at the out-
set thank all the citizens of Lunenburg for their support of this budget. Your adequate financial support
for schools will always guarantee high quality educational experiences for our children.
All of us share in the pride of an excellent school system here in Lunenburg, and recognize that the
everyday operation of the schools takes a major portion of our finances. We know that curriculum, programs,
maintenance of facilities, discipline and the quality of life in our schools are already of high achieve-
ment, but with the increase in funding we have been able to respond to a number of problem areas.
I will divide this portion into three parts to clarify what the 10.4% increase provided in the areas of
facilities, staff, and curriculum.
FACILITIES:
1. Refinished high school gym floor.
2. Installed new lockers in boys' locker room at high school.
3. Replaced several wood shop power tools and remodeled shop space.
4. Relocated computer class at high school to a full-sized classroom.
5. Purchased several new electric typewriters for high school typing classes.
6. Renovated primary school classrooms to provide new closet space.
7. Repaired primary school roof.
8. Installed new handicap entry to the high school gym.
9. Refurbished the Brooks House.
10. Purchased new furniture for one first grade class.
11. Carpeted high school auditorium.
12. Replaced circulator pumps at high school and elementary school.
13. Major increase in maintenance of buildings account ($8,000.).
14. Major increase in heating of buildings account ($25,000.).
15. Major increase in maintenance of equipment account ($6,000.).
STAFFING:
When I presented the budget to the School Committee in January (1984), I did not include salary
increases, and left many staffing positions undetermined because of the uncertainty of available funds.
1. Additional twenty-five hours of reading and math tutoring at middle school/high school.
2. Full-time librarian to the school system.
3. Increased administrative assistants at elementary and middle schools to full-time positions.
4. Additional sixteen periods per week of guided study for middle school students.
5. Payment of $9.50/per hour for school committee approved after-school extra-curricular activities.
6. Additional 1/7th eighth grade science teacher.
7. Full-time instrumental music teacher to grades 5-12.
*8. Full-time speech and language teacher to pre-school and primary school students.
*9. Additional one-half time generic specialist teacher at primary school.
*10. Additional one-half time teacher of the hearing impaired.
*11. Additional primary school special aide to first grade.
*12. Additional middle school aide to visually impaired student.
*13. Teaching assistant to pre-school integrated program.
-92-
===PAGE 95===
*14. Additional grade one tutor.
15. Increased teacher availability to gifted and talented students at elementary school from two
afternoons per week to five afternoons per week.
16. Every staff member granted a 6% across-t-board raise.
*Related to mandated Special Education program.
CURRICULUM:
Our excellent curriculum attempts to respond to three main concerns:
A. Meet the ability needs of all of our students.
B. Provide basic skills development.
C. Provide diversity and broad exposure to prepare for citizenship and career plans.
We have made a high commitment to meet these concerns. The increase in our budget provided improved
curriculum support.
1. Completion of book purchases in Social Studies, Civics, and Revised World Cultures at the high
school.
2. New course materials in high school: Introduction to Physical and Life Science and Alternate
Physics.
3. In English at the high school--expanded book acquisitions and writing folder acquisition.
4. Major increase in teaching supply account ($20,000.).
5. Major increase in textbooks account ($6,000.).
6. Complete revision of math and writing programs at elementary school.
WARRANT ARTICLES:
The Town of Lunenburg voted for two articles to be spent on the schools.
1. $70,000. for continued replacement of high school roof.
This article allows for the continued replacement of the flat portion of the high school roof.
When this project is completed, the entire high school roof will have been replaced, except for
areas over the auditorium and gym. This project, as well as all other capital improvement pro-
jects for schools, is 65% reimbursable from the state.
2. $31,000. for a wheelchair lift installed at the high school.
This project now enables handicapped persons to have total access to every level of our high
school building. This project is 65% reimbursable from the state.
THE FUTURE:
We are on the verge of unprecedented attention to public schools in this country. The care and concern
given to this topic nationwide gives credence to the pride that Americans take in their public schoolsystem.
In Lunenburg, excellent schools have been a tradition. Excellent schools, even as a tradition just do not
happen or continue to exist. They require the deliberate, long-range planning of a community. The citizens
of Lunenburg have always realized the impact that excellent schools have on the value of their real estate.
The existence of quality schools should have higher ideals. It is these ideals that I direct the future
goals of the system.
1. In Lunenburg, each child will receive the maximum attention available to develop his/her talents to
the fullest.
2. We will continue to maintain our facilities so as to provide an excellent quality of life for the
students attending and also to protect the investment this town has made.
3. We will provide avenues of communication for all citizens, parents, students and staff for input as
to the direction they wish the system to proceed.
4. Each adult working within the Lunenburg Public Schools will have one purpose in mind. They are here
to provide caring service to the children who attend.
5. The administration will provide the community with adequate and well documented information as to
potential growth and development of the school system.
CONCLUSION:
The citizens of Lunenburg must be informed as to the quality of their school committee members.
Running a four and one-half million dollar operation is no easy task. Hours are spent in reviewing material,
formulating decisions, negotiating contracts, solving problems and reviewing bids. I must pay high compli-
ments to the school committee members who work with me in carrying out the goals of this school system.
They are truly dedicated and caring individuals. To my fellow administrators, I must thank them for putting
up with the demands from central administration in addition to the desires and needs of their staff and
-93-

students. They are truly professionals and deserve the respect and praise from everyone for their dedica-
tion. Once again, a very dedicated staff must be recognized. We in Lunenburg must appreciate the efforts of
the individuals who toil with the struggle of "teaching". Each day brings an opportunity to further a
child's knowledge. The enormous responsibility this presents alone requires us to recognize their talents.

personnel, many thanks for their dedication and love for our children.
SPECIAL TRIBUTE:
I must pay special recognition to Mrs. "Ginny" Welch who
will be retiring next December after forty-two years of
continuous service to the Lunenburg Public Schools. To list
all of the attributes of this fine lady would be a long and
lengthy report in itself. We will be losing a valued town
employee. Her knowledge of the school system, the town and
its citizens and in particular her knowledge of history can-
not be replaced. In addition, I and everyone who has worked
with her will miss a dear friend. She has been through all
of the ups and downs of the school system growth and needs.
She has experienced them first-hand through superintendents,
principals, teachers, students, staff, and townsfolk.
Through it all, Ginny has remained a true professional and
dedicated friend to this school system. I will miss her
very much. I wish all the best to her and her husband, Bob,
for the time that they have worked so hard to reach. Our
love and happiness go to both of them.
VIRGINIA F. WELCH
Executive Secretary to the
Superintendent of Schools
No-School Signals - 1984-85
The decision to close schools is made by the Superintendent of Schools after consulting with the High-
way Superintendent and the Police Department to determine the condition of the roads. The practice is to
close schools only in case of extreme weather conditions or unforeseen emergency; therefore, when schools
are in session on stormy days, parents are urged to exercise their personal judgment as to the wisdom of
sending their children to school.
The following schedule of No-School Signals will be in effect for the current school year:
TWO SETS OF TWO (2) BLASTS ON THE HORN AT THE CENTRAL FIRE STATION INDICATES "NO SCHOOL" AT THE
FOLLOWING TIMES FOR THE GROUPS INDICATED:
6:30 AM
Grades 6 through 12
7:15 AM
Grades Kindergarten through 5, including Pre-School
10:00 AM
PM Session of Kindergarten and Pre-School
Announcements will be carried on the regular broadcasts of radio stations WEIM and WFGL in Fitchburg;
WLMS in Leominster; as well as WSRS, WTAG and WFTQ in Worcester; and WBZ, WHDH and WROR in Boston; and WVBF
in Framingham, as promptly as the decision is made.
The cancelling of school sessions requires the completion of numerous separate telephone calls as
rapidly as possible. The crowding of telephone circuits can mean that the signal center or bus contractor
might not be notified in time. Parents can help, by not allowing their children to make unnecessary calls.
The cooperation and understanding by all will be very much appreciated.

Report of
THE HIGH SCHOOL BEYOND THE CLASSROOM:
High School Principal
Most of us have pleasant memories of high school experiences
which enhance the satisfaction of learning and the central purposes
leading to a diploma. Lunenburg High School offers our students
many important learning and social development experiences outside
the classroom.
These are often called extra-curricular activities. Students
enjoy' them, and realize in later years, how much extra was learned.
Parents appreciate the constructive use of adolescent time and
energy, and the positive attitudes usually stimulated in their
children. Teachers and other adults contribute much time and energy
to make extra-curricular experiences possible. School administra-
tors wish they had more staff and budget resources to offer even
more activities, since these activities are expected by the commun-
ity on an on-going basis.
Close to the curriculum, a number of organizations offer
developmental experiences. The Math Team, coached by Mrs. Jean
Koykka, competes in the region against other schools, in matches to
accurately solve unique math problems in a contest form. The North-
ern Worcester County Math Leagues awards a team trophy for the year.
Lunenburg students regularly are among the top ten scorers.
A. RAY PETERSON, Ed. D.
Principal
