In support of academic courses in citizenship and government, the school maintains an extensive Student
Government. Each class has four elected officers who direct the activities of the class. For the entire
school, the Student Council consists of representatives from each of the four classes, with school-wide offi-
cers elected at-large. Within the Student Council, a Student Advisory Committee exists, which meets periodi-
cally with the Principal, to discuss school programs and policies. The Student Council advisor for several
years has been Mr. James Besarkarski. The high school also has a Youth and Government Club which provides
additional experiences with state and local government outside the school; this group has been advised by Mr.
Ron Lamothe.
For the past two years the high school has been
enthusiastically participating in the Lunenburg Public
Schools Arts Fair in the spring. This outdoor event
allows display of projects and work developed in the
high school departments of Industrial Arts, VisualArts,
Music and Physical Education. In addition, individual
classes have sponsored parent barbeques in theevening,
and in 1984, the Competitive Class Plays were offered
as a dramatic adjunct to the Arts Fair. We invite
parents and all members of the community to join us in
May for this gala annual event.
In support of extra-curricular activities, the
high school has developed a twice monthly Activity
Period on Friday. During an eight period day, the
school allows groups and organizations to meet one
period; officers in student government and organiza-
tions can plan, consult with members, or meet with
faculty, without missing regular academic classes.
Crowds gather in front of Lunenburg High
School for the Arts Fair.
Philanthropic Activities. Part of the extra-
curricular program at the high school is designed to
assist students to look outside their own adolescent
interests and think of providing services to others. This is put into practice in a number of drives to pro-
vide support for charitable activities. In 1983-84, the basketball cheerleaders under the leadership of Mrs.
Sandy Pittman, raised $1000. (which is an immense sum to be raised by a group of less than a dozen young
women). These funds were donated outright to the Leukemia Research Foundation, in an extraordinary demon-
stration of generosity. In 1984, also, the student body broke the previous high mark for contributions in
December to the Lunenburg Community Council, with a gift of $500. Each year, the National Honor Society organ-
izes a blood bank among students and faculty for contributions of blood to the American Red Cross. The
National Honor Society also works on a Heart Fund telethon, and organizes an elementary school Christmas
party. The Student Council organized an assembly presentation on the 1984 drought and starvation in Ethiopia
and other parts of Africa; this educational effort, in cooperation with Oxfam America, will culminate in a
fund raising effort.
Outside of activities related to the classroom, Lunenburg High School also offers students anopportunity
to develop outdoor recreational skills. The Skiing Program provides an opportunity for instruction in down-
hill skiing at local ski areas, along with the socialization of a pleasant outdoor event after school. The
six or SO annual trips have been chaperoned by Miss Sheila Flynn.
For social activity, the student organizations sponsor four to six dances each year, in the gym or cafe-
teria. In a community with few other recreational facilities, these events, with live or recorded music, have
been well attended and increasingly popular. Chaperoned by faculty and parents in an era when many schools
have abandoned dances, students have been benefitted from the enthusiastic and positive socialization.
Finally, in the extra-curricular programs, athletics play a prominent part. Each year more than 60% of
the high school students participate in one or more interscholastic athletic seasons. This is an excellent
percentage of participation and a tribute to the quality of the coaching staff, and to the community support
for these programs. We have been pleased in the last two years to restore several sub-varsity teams, allowing
junior varsity, and in some sports, freshmen contests to resume. This restoration has been possible through
the generosity of parent support groups in soccer, softball, and baseball. Other positions have been renewed
through the use of increased funds from gate receipts, reflecting community support, and user fees. Without
substantial cooperation from parents, school committee, students, and coaches, the athletic program at Lunen-
burg High School would not be possible.
Related to the athletic program are large groups of cheerleaders who lead spectator enthusiasm at con-
tests in football, hockey and basketball. A total of at least fifty students annually are involved in cheer-
ing, supported by several chaperones and coaches. The chaperone group has included Mrs. Sandy Pittman, Mrs.
Linda Storms, and Miss Joan Larsen.
-97-
===PAGE 100===
DEVELOPMENTS IN THE HIGH SCHOOL CURRICULUM:
The English Department has continued its emphasis on developing writing through the offering in this
school year of a one semester Writing Workshop course. Here students can get intensive faculty assistance
with creative and expository writing. Another objective of this course is an attempt to reestablish the
school literary magazine, and the course has been enthusiastically received by students and parents. Another
new offering is an English Department course in Accelerated Reading. This semester course answers the need
for motivated students to increase their rate in reading, as well as comprehension skills which have life-
long benefits.
The Business Department has received a federal grant to expand the offerings in Business and Office
Practice. Utilizing newly acquired electronic typewriters, new calculators, and transcription hardware, stu-
dents will be better prepared to undertake automated office work leaving high school.
The Science Department is offering a newly developed grade nine course, and Introduction to Physicaland
Biological Science. This course is designed to provide more science experience for those students who might
not otherwise enroll in this department as freshmen. Utilizing a number of high interest laboratory units,
the course presents the scientific backgrounds of issues and problems which are familiar to students intheir
daily lives as citizens and consumers. This course is part of the general academic program emphasis in grade
nine. It is also hoped that it will lead to an increase in student interest in later courses such as Chem-
istry, Physics, and Advanced Biology.
Chris O'Brien and Clayton Beal
Mary Foley (left) and Jennifer Speck,
studying Advanced Biology.
flutists, in the Lunenburg
High School Band.
Instrumental Music. We are pleased to again have a staff member specializing in Instrumental music. Mr.
John Hemenway will bring renewed focus and intensity to instruction on specific instruments, based upon his
years of secondary school band teaching. The structure of the band in a small school will be studied by Mr.
Hemenway, and discussed with parents and school administration, and school committee.
General Organization of Curriculum. Fall 1984 was the first year of the newly approved distribution
requirements for all students. Under these requirements, the ninth grade year is designed to be a general
academic year with courses in basic skills emphasized and specialization avoided. In the sophomore, junior
and senior years, students are required to continue to select courses from a wide variety of the school's
departments, avoiding over-specialization or vocational preparation at the early stages. At the same time,
all students are required to select exploratory courses to the extent of two semester courses during the
four years, in areas such as Art, Music, Business, Industrial Arts, and other areas of specific interest.
Graduation Requirements. The high school is also reviewing its requirements in various academic disci-
plines and total credits required for graduation. A committee of parents, students, and faculty submitted
an interim report to the Superintendent in academic year 1983-84. This committee work will continue in aca-
demic year 1984-85 with a view toward providing Lunenburg High School with the most demanding course require-
ments and total credit obligation which is consistent with a solid education in a comprehensive high school.

Report of Middle School Principal
CAROL A. EATON
JEFFREY B. O'NEILL
Principal
Administrative Assistant
Beginning the school year in September 1984, the middle school student enrollment was:
Grade 8 - 148
Grade 7 - 146
Grade 6 - 116
TOTAL - 410
During the course of the year, we gained new students, causing enrollment to reach 420 students in
January 1985. Our student population has been slowly declining over the last four years due to smaller
classes moving from the fifth grade to the sixth. The need to assess student needs and then provide for
those needs does not diminish when enrollment declines. Hopefully in the next two to four years, we will be
able to improve our student/teacher ratios and stabilize very constructive programs which are in progress.
Assessment and revision of nearly all curricular areas are the projects we are endeavoring to complete.
Improving our own accountability for curriculum and instruction is a high priority for us. We are attempt-
ing to develop a strong curricular program in microcomputer applications. We are in the developmental
stages of producing an enrichment program at the middle school. All of these areas are ambitious and require
time and energy of our current staff.
Hardworking student experiences
microcomputer.
Strong skill building in eighth grade.
One of the most important considerations is the coordination of all curriculum development with the
elementary and high schools. In a smaller school system, such as Lunenburg, we are fortunate enough to be
able to work together in our curriculum projects. Each of the schools has very experienced teachers who are
in an excellent position to shape curriculum.
CURRICULAR PROGRAM:
The middle school program is a blend of academic content acquisition along with experiences provided
which reinforce the content such as field trips, project work, leadership activities and independent study.
-99-
===PAGE 102===
Each of our three grade levels focuses very strongly on the basic skills of reading, writing, computation and
listening. We also provide strong skill building opportunities in the science and social studies areas at
each grade level. Students must learn note taking and outlining in order to process written work in all
subject areas. We are very fortunate to maintain our strong special area subject programs for our students.
Every middle school student is exposed to art, music, physical education, home economics, health, and indus-
lessons from our two music educators.
trial arts on a weekly basis. In addition to this, students may elect to take chorus and instrumental
A special feature of our eighth grade, which we are quite proud of, is the opportunity for hard working
students to take Spanish, French, Latin and algebra for high school credit. The teachers of these subjects
press our students with very high expectations to assure success in the second year of these subjects at the
high school. Both of our foreign language teachers, Josephine Meloon, French; and Kathy McGuigan, Latin and
Spanish, offer numerous opportunities for their students to apply their in-class learnings outside the class-
room. We take our students in French to Quebec City for three days to experience the culture and the French
language spoken in that area of Canada. Our Spanish students have gone to Spanish programs at the University
of Massachusetts in Amherst and in Boston. The Latin students participate in state and national Junior
Classical League activities, including the national convention, two years in a row. Last year's convention
was held in Richmond, Virginia, at the University of Richmond. We are most fortunate to have dedicated
teachers who spend their own time with students during weekends and vacation periods.
OF

NEWS
Foreign Language in action.
Hands-on Experience in Science.
This school year many of our students were involved in a federal grant and research study involving
microcomputers. The focus has been the application of computer technology to study skills and research
skills in several curricular areas. Judy Harper served as our coordinator for this program with the teach-
ing staff of Dick Bishop, Betty Carbone and Mary Ann Sudolnik. They developed curriculum units which employ
technology to teach basic study and research skills. Our computer lab containing twelve Apple II-e's and
two printers has stayed very busy all year. Several other teachers have actively used computers with stu-
dents to teach math and writing. All that we are currently working on with computer technology is being
built into the curriculum for the future. The teachers have planned and organized their approach to the
computers in a very thoughtful way.
To assist students in their academic achievement, we have a tutorial program in math and reading, as well
as a guided study program to structure study skills for students. Each of these programs are specifically
designed to meet needs of the individual students who receive these services. Students are taken from study
periods for this assistance, not regular classes of any kind. Approximately forty-five students receive
guided study help anywhere from one time per week to three times. Thirty students receive assistance from
our math and reading tutor who also serves the high school.
TESTING AND ASSESSMENT:
Each year the guidance department oversees and manages the standarized testing program. In the fall of
each year, Miriam Light and sixth and seventh grade teachers administer the Stanford Achievement Test and
then later follow-up with students and parents with the results. This is most helpful in diagnosing student
learning needs. The eighth grade students are given the Differential Aptitude Test in November each year.
Tony Falcone uses these test results to assist students in the assessment of strengths and weaknesses in
their learning abilities. This information from testing aids placement of students at each grade level as
well as entry into the high school. Students need to be aware of their performance and ability in order to
work and testing. more realistically. Teachers work toward the expansion of student expectations with the help of guidance
-100-
===PAGE 103===
Our eighth grade students are tested in the basic skills areas of writing, reading, math and listening
in April each year. Miriam Light and Sandralee Turner oversee the entire process and work with our eighth
grade teachers during the testing process. When/if students fail to meet minimum standards they are placed
in intensive subject area sections at the high school to remediate the problem and then retake the test.
Much attention is given to the successful acquisition of basic skills at the middle school.
GUIDANCE:
The guidance staff works very closely with the entire school community to deliver many services, not
just test administration and interpretation. Both counselors work with parents and teachers to develop aca-
demic and social goals for students. Much communicating is done between school and home. Counselors see
students in groups and individually to discuss issues and concerns. Miriam Light assists students in grade
six and seven with the development of strong study habits and skills. She helps them look at the broad range
of careers for the future in addition to helping with values clarification in many areas. Tony Falcone plans
for the eighth grade transition to the high school each winter and spring. Students visit our high school
and Monty Tech before Tony sits and helps them determine their schedules. Both counselors are involved in
the development of new programs such as peer counseling in the system, planning for prevention of child abuse,
gifted and talented, and a beginning of an advisor/advisee system at the middle school. Each is continuously
available to students, teachers and parents.

Report of Elementary School Principal
CONCETTA A. VERGE
ROBERT FINNERON
Principal
Administrative Assistant
Enrollment at the Primary and Elementary Schools has increased this year. As of February 1984, there
were six hundred forty-six (646) students in attendance, grades pre-school through five. As of February
1985, there are six hundred sixty-seven (667) children in attendance at those same levels.
-103-
===PAGE 106===
The grade level distribution is listed below:
Pre-School - 29 students
Grade three - 85 students
Kindergarten - 100 students
Grade four - 110 students
Grade one - 124 students
Grade five - 113 students
Grade two - 106 students
TOTAL: 667 students
Although this reflects an increase of twenty-one (21)
students, we are not sure whether the enrollment is truly
on the incline, or whether the addition of a second pre-
school session, and an unanticipated influx of first grade
enrollees during the summer of 1984 has given us a false
sense of increase. Currently, the Elementary School is in
the process of studying future enrollment, in order to
determine if in fact this pattern of increased enrollment
is a legitimate trend.
Class size in all grades, except for grade one, is
under twenty-five students per classroom. This has been a
goal of the school committee since 1981. Although we have
classes of twenty-eight and twenty-nine at the first grade
level, support services have been established at that
level, in order to insure as much attention to individual
needs as possible.

PAT FREEMAN - completed twenty years as physical education teacher for the Lunenburg School System. She
received her bachelor's degree from Tufts University.
Pat spent most of her teaching day with the children at the Primary School. She became one of
the "feature attractions" at that building, having a very positive rapport with her young
charges. Not only was Pat's program meaningful, but most enjoyable as well. The children and
staff miss Pat Freeman and the enthusiasm and sincere interest she brought to physical educa-
tion, grades pre-school through two.
DON KENNEY - a favorite of the children at the Passios School, has chosen to move to a warmer climate, and is

We all miss Don, his helpfulness and warm sense of humor.
NOREEN KITTREDGE - came to the first grade in 1953 and has been in the Lunenburg system for all but five
years of the interim. She received her bachelor's and master's degrees from Fitchburg State
College.
Noreen, who is noted for her gregarious nature, enthusiasm for teaching, and sincere interest
and involvement with her students, leaves a 'void at the Primary School. Her decision to retire
has impacted on the children, parents, and fellow-staff.
SALLY McCUE - began in Lunenburg in 1959 as a grade five teacher and served as Annex Building Supervisor for
nearly five years. She received her bachelor's degree from Fitchburg State College and leaves
the fourth grade after twenty-five years in the Lunenburg School System.
During her years of service in the Lunenburg School System, Sally served on many curriculum and
program development committees. Her valued input is missed. Also missed is Sally's vibrant
personality and mischievous sense of humor.
LYNNE THOMAS - is a graduate of the Leominster Hospital School of Nursing. Before she came to Lunenburg in
September of 1982, Lynne was a member of the Leominster Visiting Nurses Association and served
as staff nurse at the Leominster Hospital.
We enjoyed the touch of humor Lynne brought with her to the position she took so seriously.
Children and staff alike miss this warm and caring individual, who was always ready to receive
a patient with a smile.
STAFF CHANGES AND REASSIGNMENTS:
As the enrollment changes, retirements occur, or new priorities emerge, staff is often reassigned. This
year we have made the following changes in order to meet the needs of our student body:
At the Primary School -
Mrs. Janet Maki, who previously served as half-time pre-school teacher, part-time resource room teacher,
assumed the role of full-time pre-school teacher. The pre-school program was expanded to two sessions this
year. This expansion of the "tuition-based" program was made as a result of several requests by parents to
have their children involved in the Lunenburg Pre-School. Our enrollment in pre-school this year is twenty-
nine (29), as compared to last year's enrollment of fourteen (14).
Miss Susan Petrowicz, who formerly served as learning specialist at the Middle School has joined the
Primary School staff. She is replacing Mrs. Maki as half-time resource room teacher, by becoming half-time
generic specialist. Miss Petrowicz also absorbed the position of half-time kindergarten teacher. This
position became available as a result of a retirement and the reshuffling of staff, Miss Petrowicz, a highly
qualified professional, is certified as a generic specialist and in early childhood. She serves in both
regular and special education roles. We are most fortunate to have been able to include her in our staffing
this year.
Mrs. Margaret Niemi, who served last year as a grade three teacher, has assumed the position of first
grade teacher, which was made vacant by Mrs. Kittredge's retirement.
Miss Sandra Reed has replaced retiree, Mrs. Freeman, as physical education instructor for pre-school
through grade two. Although a newcomer to the Lunenburg School System, Miss Reed has had several years of
experience as a physical education instructor. She has worked very successfully with students of all ages.
At the Elementary School -
Mrs. Sheilah Cosimi, formerly half-time kindergarten teacher, half-time Chapter I tutor at the Primary
School, has assumed the role of second grade teacher. This in-house transfer came about as a result of a
retirement and a redistribution of grade level staff.
Mr. Robert Bourque returned from a one year leave-of-absence to assume the position of grade four
teacher.
-105-
===PAGE 108===
Miss Carol McManus has become half-time resource person for the Gifted and Talented Program. She
formerly served in this capacity for two-fifths of her schedule. Miss McManus also continues as half-time
fourth grade teacher.
Mrs. Nancy Rahaim, formerly full-time "Head Chapter I Tutor", is now serving as half-time substitute for
the afternoon session of Miss McManus' fourth grade class. Mrs. Rahaim continues to serve in Chapter I dur-
ing the morning.
Mrs. Jane Murray, primary resource room teacher, is on a "parent leave-of-absence" for school year
1984-85.
Mrs. Barbara Kondig and Mrs. Diane Shepard, previously primary resource room tutors, have assumed the
role of primary resource room teachers. They each serve as half-time teachers, replacing the one full-time
teacher, Mrs. Murray, who is on leave.
Mr. Donald Adams, who has served as choral and instrumental music teacher at both the middle and high
schools, has become a six-sevenths music instructor at the elementary and primary schools. He is replacing
Mrs. Kathy Lowe, who is now school librarian. Mr. Adams still serves as choral instructor for one period
each day at Lunenburg High School.
Mr. John Hemenway, another newcomer to the Lunenburg School System, has assumed the role of instrumental
music teacher, grades 5-12. Mr. Hemenway teaches instrumental music one period a day (before school) to
interested fifth grade students. He brings a great deal of experience and talent to this re-established
teaching position.
Mrs. Kathy Lowe, formerly full-time music teacher, pre-school through grade five, is now serving as
school librarian, grades two through twelve. Mrs. Lowe, a certified media specialist and school librarian,
distributes her time and expertise equally among the elementary, middle, and high schools.
Mr. Robert Finneron, who last year spent two afternoons a week in the classroom, in order to relieve
Miss McManus for the Gifted and Talented program, has returned to his position of full-time administrative
assistant.
Mrs. Kim Howard joins us as school nurse, replacing Mrs. Lynne Thomas, who retired last year. Mrs.
Howard, a Registered Nurse, has had experience in Boston and other smaller communities.
UPDATE ON THE ACADEMICS:
Having received our most recent standardized test results, and having interpreted those results, we are
most pleased to report that the students at. the T. C. Passios Elementary School compare very well to the top
third of the nation, academically. Scores in the basic skill areas of reading, math, language, and writing
are very high.
First graders "drilled" in reading skills.
Fifth graders "boning up" in the school library.
This information assures us that the programs incorporated in the school have been very effective. It
also indicates that we should continue in the direction we have been pursuing. Therefore, we will continue
to stress carefully monitored instruction in reading, math, writing, and mechanics usage, incorporating the
use of Achievement Monitoring Programs (AMP), writing rating systems, and reading management systems.
Since work has been completed in putting those programs into place, we are now directing our attention
toward the content areas of science and social studies. This year we are studying our social studies curric-
ulum and text. We are evaluating both, with the intent of revision of the kindergarten through grade five
curriculum. A new text was purchased for school year 1984-85. The task of the staff and administration is
to insure that a sequential, logical, K-5 program is established, maximizing the use of the new text and
incorporating meaningful supplementals.
-106-
===PAGE 109===
In the area of science, we are investigating means by which we can supplement our current program,
emphasizing process (hands-on activities, experiments, demonstrations, laboratories) in order to augment the
content presented. Audio-visual materials, laboratory supplies, and science charts and models are being con-
sidered for purchase.
While maintaining a "watchful eye" on basic skill areas, we will simultaneously pursue a means of maxi-
mizing excellence in the content area programs.
LUNENBURG PRE-SCHOOL:
The Lunenburg Pre-School, Lunenburg Early Intervention
Program (LEIP) which is housed in the Primary School, has
been expanded this year. As of September 1984, the Pre-
School, formerly a one-session a day program, was extended
to two sessions. Following the same hourly schedule as the
kindergarten program, the Pre-School boasts enrollment of
sixteen children in the morning session and thirteen chil-
dren in the afternoon session.
Not all Pre-Schoolers attend school five days a week.
Some of the youngsters' parents have opted for a partial
program of either two or three days each week. However,
the majority of children are involved in the five day pro-
gram.
This tuition-based program, under the direction of
Mrs. Janet Maki, concentrates on socialization skills,
behavior management, language development, and pre-academic
Pre-Schoolers involved in
readiness. Mrs. Maki, and teaching assistant, Lisa Stone,
activity with teaching staff.
incorporate a great deal of music, physical education, and
arts and crafts in the program.
In addition to attempting to prepare the young child to succeed academically in the ensuing school
years, the teachers address the development of the total child, hoping to instill a sense of self-confidence,
self-worth, and self-awareness in each of the youngsters.
Parent involvement and interaction with the pre-school teachers is another important component of LEIP.
Workshops concerning early childhood issues are offered to parents. Individual parent conferences and/or
home visits are often made by pre-school staff, in order to deal with specific issues.
Meaningful early childhood education is critical to the academic, social, and emotional development of
the child. It is our aim to provide top quality early childhood education through the Lunenburg Early Inter-
vention Program, in order to maximize the opportunity for each child to meet his/her potential, and lay a
foundation for a productive, successful educational experience in the Lunenburg Public Schools.
VOLUNTEERS, AIDES, TUTORS, ALL A BIG HELP AT THE PRIMARY AND ELEMENTARY SCHOOLS:
Parent, student, and community support and assistance at the elementary level has been overwhelming, and
very much appreciated. Many of the programs at our schools owe their smooth and fine functioning, in part,
to the assistance of these groups.
Students from Lunenburg High School have been involved with elementary children, serving in the capaci-
ties of aides, assistants, and tutors.
Working with Mr. Richard Mulligan are a group of high schoolers who serve as physical education aides.
They assist students who are having difficulty with particular skills. They provide safety coverage, referee
game activities, and follow-up on teacher instruction.
Working with Mr. Allan Wilson, Speech and Language Therapist, are a group of students from Lunenburg
High School. They serve as speech aides. Under Mr. Wilson's supervision, these trained aides provide drill
on articulation and supervise activities assigned by the therapist, while the therapist is working with other
students in the group.
Talented art students from Lunenburg High School make themselves available to Mrs. Doris Cormier, and
the children in Mrs. Cormier's art classes. The high school art aides assist individual children having
difficulty with projects, and aid the teacher in preparing materials and cleaning up, between rather tightly
scheduled art classes.
Recently, in collaboration with Mrs. Jean Koykka, high school math teacher, a Math Tutor/Teaching
Assistant Program was initiated. A group of top level high school math students have opted to become
involved with children from the Elementary School who are in either average or accelerated math groups. The
high school students, under Mrs. Koykka's supervision, work with elementary classroom teachers in providing
follow-up to classroom instruction, individual computer involvement in mathematics, and math enrichment
activities.
-107-
===PAGE 110===
The goal of the Elementary School and High School is to provide a service which is mutually beneficial
to students from both levels. Students from the|High school level benefit from the interaction with young
children, and from their involvement in the educational arena, under the supervision of experienced elemen-
tary teachers. Aides and tutors from the High School receive high school credit for their involvement at
the Elementary School.
The children at the elementary level, obviously,
benefit from the direction and attention given to them
by the high schoolers. There is a very positive inter-
action between the two groups. The children at the
Elementary School are in awe of their young tutors and
respond very well to the attention provided by this
group.

IN-SERVICE FOR ELEMENTARY SCHOOL CONTINUES:
In an attempt to continue providing in-service for veteran teachers, the Elementary School has estab-
lished workshops around such topics as: The "How-Tos" of Micro-Computers, Integrating Existing Software in
the Regular Curriculum, Language Development and Remediation, Science Methods and Techniques, and The School
Age Child--Kindergarten Through Grade Eight.
The varied workshops have and will be conducted during different times of the year. They have been
funded through Commonwealth In-Service Grants, Collaborative Grants from Fitchburg State College, and School
System Curriculum Development/In-Service funds.
An attempt has been made to develop in-service programs around perceived needs of the staff. Surveys
and needs assessments were utilized prior to the formulation of the workshops. By providing in-service
specific to the needs of the staff, we insure utilization of the knowledge attained by staff to the benefit
of our children.
-108-
===PAGE 111===
In addition to the in-house, in-service programs,
several staff members are pursuing formal graduate work.
However, the provision of in-service, locally, allows
more of our veteran staff to maintain current. The pro-
grams also assist the teacher by addressing the problem
specific to the children with whom they work.

COMMUNICATION CRITICAL TO THE SUCCESS OF THE
ELEMENTARY SCHOOL:
Input and feedback from parents, community members,
and staff, relative to the "goings on" at the Elementary
P. T. 0. Executive Board meets
School is a necessary component to smooth and successful
with a group of interested parents
functioning of this institution. Because of this philo-
during the first "Coffee Hour."
sophy, a series of groups, and/or interventions have
been organized and devised to facilitate communication.
One of the groups which helps facilitate communication and which provides input to the school, is the
Principal's Advisory Committee. This committee is comprised of parents, staff, and the building principal.
Committee meetings are held monthly. An agenda is. distributed to committee members prior to each meeting.
The agenda usually includes items relative to direction of the curriculum, goals of the school and/or admin-
istration, and new program ideas.
Extensive discussion is conducted around each item. The building principal serves as chairperson for
the group. However, there is always a great deal of interaction among group members.
During the discussion period, committee members make suggestions or recommendations around specific
topics. Their input is noted, and often, specific suggestions are incorporated in the final product (i.e.
design of a program, involvement with a group or organization, etc.)
The Principal's Advisory Committee members serve as conduits of information to fellow-staff, other
parents, and community members. Parent members often bring to our discussions questions or concerns relative
to our school or programs, that other parents have expressed. Staff members bring to the group's attention,
the perspective (s) of other staff members not serving on the committee.
The interchange that takesplace among members is very significant. The outcomes of the dialogues held
during the meetings are of particular value to the building principal who, in turn, sees that the Elementary
School benefits from these outcomes.
Another means of communicating with groups of parents is through the Grade Level Open House meetings.
Separate grade level meetings are set up early in the school year. Parents are invited to evening meetings.
-109-
===PAGE 112===
in order to hear presentations from administration and staff members at each grade level relative to curric-
ulum, policy, and practices. Parents are then invited to dialogue with the presenters, in order to clarify
issues and become more informed about the specific features at the particular grade level presenting.
Another means of encouraging interaction between school and community is through the "Fireside Chat"
medium. Fireside Chats are meetings scheduled and conducted by the building principal.
Parents and other community members often are confused about programs and policy in their schools. They
often have questions or concerns that go unanswered because there does not appear to be a format for group
discussion or a vehicle which they may utilize in order to learn more about school programs or to have their
questions answered.
"Fireside Chats" begin with a topic for discussion which the principal addresses. The topics are
recommended by the parents and teacher members of the Principal's Advisory Committee. Topics selected are
those perceived to be somewhat controversial in nature. This approach is taken in order to generate discus-
sion between the principal and the parents. If THE TOPIC is exhausted, other issues or concerns of the group
are discussed.
Naturally, parents are encouraged to contact school staff or administration around specific issues.
However, the informal Fireside Chats provide an additional opportunity for further communication.
In addition to coverage we are able to rely on from the press, the Elementary School has established the
policy of disseminating a monthly newsletter, "Elementary News". The distribution of "Elementary News" allows
us to reach all parents of Elementary School children with an update of events and programs at the school,
while allowing us to feature some samples of student work.
Due largely to the efforts of Mrs. Gloria O'Brien and Mrs. Peg Niemi, "Elementary News" has become a
most important vehicle by which we are able to keep large numbers of parents informed.
We feel that parents and community members have been very supportive of the school. This may, in part,
be a result of the attempt for open communication between the two groups.

AFTER-SCHOOL PROGRAMS INITIATED:
A need has long been expressed to provide organized after-school activities for the elementary school-
age child. Unfortunately, funding such programs appeared to be an impossibility. However, this year, a new
approach was taken, and a very successful, well subscribed After-School Activities program is ongoing at the
Elementary School.
During the summer and early fall, after-school activity proposals were drafted and presented to the
superintendent and school committee for their consideration. The proposals requested a minimum, school
system contribution to help support the after-school activities. However, the proposals stated that the
major portion of funds for the program would be generated by "users fees".
Since the "users fees" concept was new to the elementary level, parent and staff input was sought, as to
the feasibility of incorporating this concept to fund the program. The response from both groups was most
positive. Hence, the after-school activities on a "users fee" basis, were implemented.
Sports activities being offered this year are: soccer, hiking, cross country skiing, poly hockey,
wiffle ball, gymnastics, volleyball, T-ball, and precision rope jumping. Art activities will consist of
papier mache', cross stitch, and ceramics class.
-110-
===PAGE 113===
The After-School Activities have been a great success. Additional sessions have had to be scheduled in
order to respond to the numbers of children wishing to participate.
It is the goal of the school to teach the children meaningful skills in the arts and athletics, in
addition to providing them with worthwhile activities in which to participate after school. We feel that we
are accomplishing this through the Elementary After-School Activity Program.

SERVICES PROVIDED TO PARENTS OF ELEMENTARY STUDENTS:
In response to requests for assistance in parenting issues, Lois Davenport, Elementary School Guidance
Counselor/School Psychologist, has offered parenting workshop sessions. Mrs. Davenport, a trained Active
Parenting Program Instructor, has conducted morning and evening sessions of two-hour duration, for periods
of six weeks a workshop.
Active Parenting is a program designed to teach parents strategies to become more effective in dealing
with their children. The video tape/discussion format is non-threatening, interesting, and appealing to_
parents.
Active Parenting Workshops have been so popular with
parents that Mrs. Davenport will conduct two more six-
week sessions prior to the end of this school year.
A need has also been expressed by a group of par-
ents, for assistance with issues surrounding the "single
parent."
One way that adults may work through issues related
to their new role as single parents is through partici-
pation in a support group. In such a group, it is possi-
ble to share concerns about anger, loneliness, financial
responsibilities, and children's issues and engage in
creative problem-solving and the building of a support
network.
Through the efforts of Mrs. Davenport, a single
parent support group sponsored by the Herbert Lipton
Mental Health Center in cooperation with the Lunenburg
Schools has been established. Lunenburg High School
Guidance Counselor/School Psychologist Adrienne Mills,
will co-facilitate this group with the counselor from the
Mental Health Center. Parents confronted with separation
Mrs. Lois Davenport,
and divorce have been encouraged to explore the many ways
planning parent workshop
in which the group can be helpful.
with the building principal.
"Education is really not a process of instilling something in us from the outside. Its true purpose is
to reveal something already latent within us. True education is more than imparting knowledge.
It is the arousing and awakening in ourselves of inherent wisdom which will illumine the mind."

In Memory Of
ROBERT C. COLEMAN
Less than a year after his retirement as primary school custodian, Bob Coleman passed away on May 13,
1984 in the University Hospital in Worcester. As well as being a custodian with the school department, he
also owned and operated a sewing machine repair business for many years. In addition, Mr. Coleman was a
well-known musician, playing as a bassist through the north county with the George Nyman Trio for many
years. Bob was born in Kalamazoo, Michigan and lived many years in South Ashburnham and the past twenty
years in Lunenburg. He was a Marine Corps veteran of World War II and a survivor of Pearl Harbor. He left
his wife, Rita (Watt) Coleman, three sons, four daughters, seven grandchildren and several nephews and
nieces.
Retirements During 1984
SALLY A. McCUE
September 1, 1959 - June 30, 1984
Mrs. McCue is a graduate of Fitchburg State College and had taken graduate courses at the same institu-
tion while a member of the elementary teaching staff. Sally came to Lunenburg as a teacher of grade five in
September 1959, having taught previously in East Hartford, Connecticut and Fitchburg, Massachusetts. She
served the school system over the twenty-five year period with dedication, competency, conscientiousness,
and dependability. She was very outgoing and a joy to have around. We all miss her, but hope she is enjoy-
ing her retirement to the fullest. (See also Report of Elementary Principal)
PRISCILLA A. FREEMAN
September 1, 1964 - June 30, 1984
Mrs. Freeman received her bachelor's degree from Tufts University in 1950 and taught in Athol, Sudbury,
and Groton, Massachusetts before joining the elementary staff in September 1964 as physical education
instructor. She retired in June 1984 after twenty years of dedicated service. Priscilla was soft spoken
but with definite ideas for her classes and 'a big concern for the students under her tutelage. She is
-
missed by all the children who were fortunate to have had her as a teacher. May she enjoy many years of
retirement. (See also Report of Elementary School Principal)
DONALD T. KENNEY
January 2, 1969 - June 30, 1984
Mr. Kenney became a member of the custodial staff of the Lunenburg Public Schools on January 2, 1969
when he began as a junior custodian at the high school. The following September he transferred to the
middle school until January 1971 when he transferred again to the elementary annex. In November of 1973,
Don was appointed as senior custodian at the Passios Elementary School replacing someone. who had retired.
When the senior groundsperson/utility person retired in April 1979, Don applied and was appointed to this
position. When this position was deleted in 1981, Don returned to the Passios Elementary School as senior
custodian until his retirement on June 30, 1984. Don brought to all the positions that he held a sense of
maturity, pride, concern and care which are' so essential in a school environment. Presently, Don is a
resident of Englewood, Florida, enjoying his retirement to the fullest, we are sure. (See also Report
of
Elementary School Principal)
M. NOREEN KITTREDGE
September 1953 - June 30, 1955;
January 1960 - June 30, 1984
Mrs. Kittredge received her bachelor's and master's degrees from Fitchburg State College and during her
tenure in Lunenburg, completed thirty hours credit beyond her master's.
Noreen came to Lunenburg in September 1953 as a teacher of grade one, having taught the previous year
in Springfield, Massachusetts. In June of 1955, Noreen resigned to accept the position of Supervisor at the
Fitchburg State College Training School. However, she returned to Lunenburg again in January of 1960 to
finish out the school year in grade three. In September of the same year, she transferred to a grade one
position and taught at this grade level until her retirement in June 1984. Noreen was a sincere and
committed individual and a highly sensitive, warm and caring person. She is missed by all; we wish her the
best in her years of retirement. (See also Report of Elementary Principal)
